Task: hybrid MPI + OpenMP + CUDA for max performance and scalability, correctness and equivalent semantics.\n\nWe need to design a hybrid Floyd-Warshall. Standard approach:\n\nData layout is column-major (idx2(i,j)=j*n+i), so dist[i][j] at j*n+i. The reference loop order: k outer, i inner-ish, j innermost. Actually with column-major, dist[j][i] = column j (i varies contiguous). Let's think about the semantics: dist[idx2(j,i,n)] = dist[i][j] logically? idx2(i,j) = j*n+i means element at row i, column j in column-major. In floydWarshall, distIJ = dist[idx2(j,i,n)] = element row j, column i => dist[j][i]. distIK = dist[idx2(k,i,n)] = dist[k][i]. distKJ = dist[idx2(j,k,n)] = dist[j][k]. Hmm so the update is dist[j][i] = min(dist[j][i], dist[k][i] + dist[j][k])? Wait that doesn't match standard Floyd Warshall. Let's check semantics with index naming: maybe they interpret idx2(i,j) where the first arg is column? Actually standard FW: d[i][j] = min(d[i][j], d[i][k]+d[k][j]). Here they compute d[j][i] = min(d[j][i], d[k][i] + d[j][k]). This is equivalent to standard FW transposed (since d[k][i] = d[i][k] logically). Since the matrix is symmetric in initialization (random symmetric-ish? Actually initialization uses rand per element so not symmetric). Hmm.\n\nBut we only need to reproduce the exact same loop semantics, preserving the exact result (hash). Simplest: keep the loop structure but parallelize.\n\nWe need to be careful: the outer loop order is k, then i, then j. For GPU, typical FW: each k, update whole matrix on GPU with a kernel iterating over i,j; parallelism is over i,j (n^2), with k sequential. That's standard and results identical to the triple loop order as long as k sequential and no race: for fixed k, each (i,j) element is updated independently reading row/col k which is not modified during iteration k? Wait distIK = dist[k][i] = element (row k, col i) or row? Let's map indices: in kernel for fixed k, we read dist[idx2(k,i)] for all i and dist[idx2(j,k)] for all j. We write dist[idx2(j,i)]. Is any written element also read? Written: idx2(j,i) for all i,j. Read dist[idx2(k,i)] for i all — these are elements idx2(k,i), i.e., row k or column? idx2(a,b)=b*n+a. Read set 1 = {i*n + k : all i} — column k of the memory matrix (contiguous stride n). Read set 2 = {k*n+j : all j} — row k contiguous. Written set = all. Overlaps: when written element idx2(j,i) with j=k -> k*n+i (read set 2); when i=k -> j*n+k (read set 1). So writes to row k / column k could race with reads by other threads. But also in the sequential algorithm, during iteration k, updates to row/column k: for i=k, distIJ = dist[j][k], distIK = dist[k][k]=0, distKJ = dist[j][k], newDist = dist[j][k], not <, so no change. Similarly j=k: dist[k][i] unchanged. So row/column k are not modified in iteration k. Good. So parallelizing over all (i,j) for fixed k is safe even in-place, as long as we read row/col k. But with the transposed mapping, the \"read rows\" are row k and column k of memory, both unchanged. Great.\n\nAlso path update: path[idx2(j,i)] = k when update. That's fine.\n\nBut wait, the exact semantics: loops i then j, but since updates independent for fixed k and row/col k unchanged during iteration, the order doesn't matter. So a GPU kernel over all (j,i) gives the same result exactly.\n\nNow hybrid MPI: Partition matrix among MPI ranks. Standard approach for distributed FW that avoids replicating whole matrix: 2D block distribution. For each k, the rank that owns row/col k broadcasts pivot row and column; all ranks update their local blocks. This requires gathering row/col k. With column-major memory, \"row k\" and \"column k\" both needed (dist[k][i] and dist[j][k] with their naming). Let's define logical matrix D where element at memory idx2(a,b) = D[a][b]? idx2(a,b)=b*n+a, so memory index for (a,b) is b*n+a, i.e., column-major with first index a as row. Let's define D[a][b] at memory idx2(a,b). Then update: D[j][i] = min(D[j][i], D[k][i] + D[j][k]) — this is D_ji = min(D_ji, D_ki + D_jk), which is standard FW on transposed matrix D^T: (D^T)[i][j] = min((D^T)[i][j], (D^T)[i][k]+(D^T)[k][j]). Yes exactly. So equivalent to standard FW on D^T where memory is row-major for D^T. Fine.\n\nFor distributed with 2D block distribution of D or of memory matrix, pivot row and column in terms of D^T: update for fixed k uses row k of D^T (D^T[k][j] = D[j][k]) and column k (D^T[i][k] = D[k][i]). In memory terms, D^T row k contiguous? D^T[k][j] = D[j][k] = memory idx2(j,k) = k*n+j contiguous. Yes that's memory row k contiguous. D^T column k = D[k][i] = idx2(k,i)=i*n+k, strided. So pivot row is memory row k (contiguous), pivot column is memory column k (strided). This matches standard.\n\nDesign choices for hybrid: MPI across nodes, each rank handles a 2D block, OpenMP and CUDA within rank. With GPUs, ideally one MPI rank per GPU. 4 GPUs. We want max performance and scalability.\n\nSimplest robust high-performance design that supports arbitrary ranks: 1D block distribution along rows of D^T. But 2D is better for communication volume.\n\nActually there's a simpler approach that's quite scalable: each MPI rank owns a block of columns of the memory matrix (which corresponds to rows of D^T?) Hmm. Let me think about what's easiest to implement correctly and still fast: replicate full O(n^2) matrix per rank? That doesn't scale to large n but for benchmark sizes maybe. But requirement says \"maximum parallel scalability\", \"accelerator cluster\". Better use 2D block-cyclic or block distribution.\n\nHowever, correctness/time is limited. Let me consider a well-known scheme: 2D block distribution with block size chosen so blocks are cache/GPU friendly, and each rank needs pivot row and column broadcast each iteration. Communication: for each k, the owner of row k broadcasts n elements (the pivot row) and owner of column k broadcasts n elements. Actually if the matrix is distributed by 2D blocks, the pivot row k is distributed across the block-column of process rank. To broadcast full pivot row, need all ranks holding pieces of row k to broadcast; standard approach: for each k, the column of processes that owns row k collectively broadcast row segments to processes in same block-row. With MPI, easiest: gather row/column k to all ranks via MPI_Allgather of pieces, or use subcommunicators.\n\nSimpler: Use 1D distribution by columns of D^T (i.e., memory rows). Then each rank owns D^T[:, block] = memory rows block. Hmm with memory column-major, row-major blocks of memory = D^T blocks of rows. Pivot row k of D^T is memory row k, owned entirely by one rank; broadcast to all ranks (MPI_Bcast of n elements). Pivot column k of D^T is memory column k, spread across ranks in segments of size blocksize; use MPI_Allgather with variable counts or gatherv, then distribute. Or each rank has the segment of column k in its local block, allgather to all: MPI_Allgatherv n elements. That's 1D block distribution along rows of D^T. Each rank updates rows subset: for each i in local block rows, for each j: D^T[i][j] = min(D^T[i][j], D^T[i][k] + D^T[k][j]). Pivot row D^T[k][:] is one contiguous memory row, broadcast by owner. Pivot column D^T[:,k] = D^T[i][k] for i in local rows, contiguous in memory (stride? D^T[i][k] = memory k*n+i, for i in block -> contiguous block!). Because memory is column-major, column k of D^T is contiguous memory segment k*n + i for i in row-block. Great. So 1D block distribution over rows of D^T gives cheap allgather of column k: exactly the local block segment contiguous, n/numranks elements per rank. And pivot row k is n contiguous elements owned by single rank.\n\nThat's actually 1D row distribution of D^T = column distribution of D. Update: each rank owns a set of rows of D^T (a set of \"i\" values, which are the second index in D? Let's re-derive in memory: D^T row i is memory column i? Wait no. Memory index idx2(a,b) = b*n+a. If we call memory linear array M, M[b*n+a] = D[a][b]. A contiguous chunk of M of length n is M[r*n .. r*n+n-1] = D[0..n-1][r] = column r of D = row r of D^T. So memory row r = D^T row r. Good. So distributing contiguous chunks of the flat array = distributing rows of D^T. Element M[r*n + c] = D^T[r][c]. In the original code, distIJ = dist[idx2(j,i)] = M[i*n+j] = D^T[i][j]. distIK = dist[idx2(k,i)] = M[i*n+k] = D^T[i][k]. distKJ = dist[idx2(j,k)] = M[k*n+j] = D^T[k][j]. Update: D^T[i][j] = min(D^T[i][j], D^T[i][k] + D^T[k][j]). Yes! Standard FW on D^T with memory row-major. \n\nSo distribution: partition rows of D^T (contiguous chunks of flat memory) among ranks. Each rank owns rows [r0, r1). For each k:\n- Need full pivot row k: D^T[k][j] for all j = M[k*n + j]. Owned entirely by rank owning row k. Broadcast (n elements) from owner to all ranks.\n- Need pivot column k elements for local rows: D^T[i][k] = M[i*n + k] for i in [r0,r1). Allgather: each rank contributes its local segment of length (r1-r0) at offset r0.\nThen update local rows: for i in [r0,r1), for j in 0..n-1: val = D^T[i][k] + pivotRow[j]; if val < D^T[i][j]: update dist and path = k. This is exactly correct, preserves semantics (for fixed k writes to rows, reads pivot row k and column k values which are not written... wait if local rows include row k, do we write row k? Update for i=k: D^T[k][j] = min(D^T[k][j], D^T[k][k] + D^T[k][j]) = unchanged since D^T[k][k]=0. But in parallel, i=k updates are no-ops, so fine. But reading pivotRow precomputed ensures row k stays static. What about D^T[i][k] for i local: if i=k, pivot col value is D^T[k][k]=0; writes to column k of local rows? Updates write D^T[i][j] for all j including j=k. For j=k: D^T[i][k] = min(D^T[i][k], D^T[i][k] + D^T[k][k]) = unchanged. So no actual change. Good.\n\nSo this 1D scheme is simple, exactly equivalent, and communication per iteration is O(n) broadcast + O(n) allgather = O(n) per rank; total O(n^2) communication, which is optimal-ish. Computation per rank O(n^3 / P) where P=#ranks. Scaling good.\n\nBut load balance: each rank has n/P rows; fine.\n\nNow within each rank we have a contiguous block of rows (local n/P x n). Use CUDA GPU to do update: kernel launches for each k, with grid over local rows × columns, reading pivotRow (n) and pivotCol (local rows). Number of kernel launches = n (512 or more), each launch small (for n=512, P=4 ranks → local 128x512 = 65536 threads). Launch overhead ~5-10us * 512 iterations = few ms. Each rank local rows.\n  \n  Communication per block: broadcast diagonal block b×b (owner → all), broadcast pivot rows b×n (owner → all). That's 2 collectives per block, P blocks total. Total comm volume: P*(b² + bn) = n² + nb. For P=4, b=n/4, that's huge: b*n per block * 4 blocks = n² (same total), and only 4 rounds. Bcast message size b*n = n²/4 (for 4096 → 4M elements = 16MB per block) for 4 rounds. Bandwidth-wise, 64MB total per rank. At shared memory bandwidth ~10GB/s effective Bcast, ~6ms. Latency negligible. Great.\n  \n  But phase 1 owner computing b×b diagonal with k in K must be done before phase 2. Better than 375.\n\n  But large b means phase 2 pivot block message 16MB per Bcast x 4 = 64MB, fine.\n\n  Main concern: phase 1 owner-only creates serialization. Could instead choose smaller b (e.g., 64) with many blocks; each block owned by one rank (block must not cross rank boundary); diagonal compute total = (n/b)*b³ = n*b² ops distributed across ranks. For b=64, n=4096 → 4096*4096=16.7M ops, negligible. But communication rounds = n/b=64, message size b*n=64*4096=262K elements=1MB per block for pivot row, times 64 = 64MB, same. Plus diagonal b²=4096 elements. Latency 64 rounds * (maybe 100us for 1MB bcast) = 6.4ms. Good. Smaller b reduces owner-idle diagonal but increases rounds. b=64 or 128 good.\n\n  But blocks must align to rank boundaries. With 1D row distribution, each rank's range may not be multiple of b. We can choose global block boundaries multiples of b, but a block crossing rank boundary would be owned by two ranks; diagonal block D[K][K] would be split. To avoid, choose per-rank block sizes: each rank partitions its own row range into chunks of at most b, with the last chunk possibly different (remainder). Then each block is within one rank. Number of blocks total = sum ceil(b_i/b) ≈ n/b. Good. Phase 2 pivot column update D[I][K] needs D[K][K] which is locally owned. Fine.\n\n  For the pivot column phase for rows outside K: each rank's local rows i need D[i][l] for l in K (columns in K) and diagonal block D[l][k]. We can compute with a kernel over local rows × b, with k loop over b, reading local dist[i*n + (K0+l)] and sDiag[l*b + (k-K0)]. O(localRows * b²) per block. Sum over blocks: localRows * b * n ≈ n²*b/P per rank; for b=64, n=4096, P=4: 4096*64*4096/4=268M ops per rank, much less than total n³/P=17G. Fine.\n\n  Phase 2 pivot row update for owner's rows K: D[i][j] = min over l in K using updated D[K][K]: O(b² n) on owner. Sum over blocks: b²n*(n/b)=n²b = 4096²*64=1.07G ops total (across owners), per rank ~0.27G. Fine.\n\n  Phase 3 off-diagonal: O(localRows * (n-b) * b)? That's n³/P, the dominant part. For each local row i, for each j not in K, min over l in K of D[i][K0+l] + D[K0+l][j]. That's b work per (i,j). Summed over blocks: localRows * n * n = n³/P per rank. Yes dominant. Good.\n\n  So total compute same order, with more kernel passes but okay.\n\n  Communication per block:\n  - Diag block b×b: owner broadcasts to all (or allgatherv).\n  - Pivot row block b×n: owner broadcasts to all.\n  Could combine into one message of b*(n+b) elements to reduce latency: pack D[K][K] (b×b) and D[K][:] (b×n) → b*(n+b). Owner has both on device/local. For non-owner, need both. We can use MPI_Bcast of b*(n+b) elements. Number of rounds n/b. For b=64, n=4096: 64 rounds of 64*(4096+64)=266K elements=1.06MB. Good.\n\n  But there's a subtlety: phase 2 order. Standard blocked FW sequence:\n  1. D[K][K] update (needs only local owner).\n  2. D[K][J] for all J ≠ K (pivot rows) using D[K][K] — owner.\n  3. D[I][K] for all I ≠ K (pivot columns) using D[K][K] and local D[I][l] — each rank local rows.\n  4. D[I][J] for I,J ≠ K using D[I][K] (updated in step3) and D[K][J] (updated in step2) — each rank local rows.\n  For correctness of step 4, do we need step 3 to happen before step 4? Step 4 could use D[i][l] directly (local columns) instead of updated D[I][K]; actually standard formula step 4: D[i][j] = min(D[i][j], min_{k in K} D[i][k] + D[k][j]). If we use the *updated* D[i][k] from step 3, that's D[i][k] after considering all K, which can include multiple K intermediates. Using it is still valid and perhaps processes more. But standard blocked FW does steps 2,3,4 each using pivot row/col. Actually the typical algorithm:\n    - Phase 1: update diagonal block.\n    - Phase 2: update pivot row/column (cross blocks adjacent to K).\n    - Phase 3: update all other blocks.\n  In phase 3, it uses the phase-2-updated pivot row and column, which incorporate the full k-block. This is correct.\n  \n  For distance exactness: yes.\n\n  Path exactness: likely differs. Accept? Hmm.\n\nGiven complexity and risk, and time, maybe stick with exact per-k implementation but optimize the H2D and maybe Bcast. Could we leverage CUDA streams to overlap H2D of pivot row with... nothing.\n\nCan we reduce H2D by keeping a device copy of the pivot row updated incrementally? Idea: Instead of copying the full pivot row from host to device each k, use mapped pinned memory for the pivot row and let the kernel read from host. Let's test empirically whether zero-copy read is fast enough. If kernel reads pivot from mapped host memory, per iteration saves 8.5us H2D but may add kernel time. Since kernel is 47us per iteration for np=4 (192ms/4096), adding host reads might increase. But pivot row is small and L2 cached; after first block reads, cached. However each k a new row, so 16KB per k from host via PCIe; latency over PCIe ~1-2us, and 128 cache lines can be prefetched concurrently. Total maybe + few us. Could net save ~5us/iter → 20ms on n=4096, and ~2ms on n=512. Worth testing but not huge.\n\nThe Bcast 34us is the biggest. Why is it 34us with pinned vs 22us pure pageable? The canonical formula for \"pivot column\" update is for each k in K: D[i][k] = min(D[i][k], min_{l in K} D[i][l]+D[l][k]) where D[i][l] are the current values (after previous blocks, and possibly after within-block updates?). This uses D[i][l] which for l in K is part of the same pivot column block and gets updated during the loop. Good.\n\nImplement:\n- Before loop, `unsigned int* h_pivotRow` etc.\n- Inside loop:\n```\nconst unsigned int* pivotDevice;\nif (size == 1) {\n   pivotDevice = d_dist + (k - r0)*numNodes; // r0=0\n   // no D2H, no comm, no H2D\n} else {\n   if (rank == owner) D2H...\n   exchange...\n   if (rank == owner) {\n       pivotDevice = d_dist + (k-r0)*numNodes;\n   } else {\n       H2D d_pivotRow; pivotDevice = d_pivotRow;\n   }\n}\n...\nkernel(..., pivotDevice, ...)\n```\nBut wait: for size==1, skip the H2D block entirely. Good. For multi-rank non-owner, H2D. For owner, no H2D.\n\nHowever, in the shared path, after the barrier all ranks read slot into h_pivotRow. The owner could skip that read-back (saves a memcpy). But code currently does memcpy for all; we can condition `if (rank != owner) memcpy(...)`. And for owner, don't need h_pivotRow after writing. Fine. For Bcast multi-node, it's collective; owner's h_pivotRow already has data (D2H) and Bcast root uses it. Then owner can skip H2D. Good.\n\nThis optimization is worthwhile and simple. Let's implement.\n\nAlso for size==1, `useShm` false. So path.\n\nNow another optimization: for size>1, when owner == rank, we do D2H then H2D? With new logic skip H2D. Good.\n\nCould we also avoid D2H for the owner by having a copy of the owner's pivot row on host maintained? No, D2H needed to share.\n\nNow consider using OpenMP for the host-side exchange in shared path? Not needed.\n\nLet's patch the loop accordingly.\n\nCurrent loop section lines ~385 onwards. {"type":"reasoning","timestamp":1792583583026,"sessionID":"ses_ea1d4e13dffe4OR63ZOm0eWLMk","part":{"id":"prt_15e4e9d61001KcwaB16KFivlna","messageID":"msg_15e4e3df4001rI5dljQCrsoVlJ","sessionID":"ses_ea1d4e13dffe4OR63ZOm0eWLMk","type":"reasoning","text":"The code looks good. One concern: In the compute loop, for `size > 1` and `bl > 0`, all ranks do exchange. For `size == 1`, no exchange; fine. But `useShm` is computed once; if `size>1` and single node. Good.\n\nNow let's think about a potential correctness bug with the owner's path matrix versus the copy's distances when the owner's normal kernel updates its rows K and other rows. The owner uses pivotDevice = d_dist row k. But the owner's row k might be modified by the normal kernel itself? We argued no. However, the normal kernel updates all local rows, and thread i=k, j varies: `dist[base+k]` = D[k][k] = 0; nd = 0 + pivotRow[j]; pivotRow = row k = dist[k*n+j]. So nd = dist[k*n+j] = current, no write. But what about path? no write. So row k stable. Good. And column k: thread i, j=k: nd = D[i][k] + pivotRow[k] = D[i][k] + D[k][k] = D[i][k], no write. So column k stable. The kernel reads pivotRow[j] which is row k; stable. So no race. Good.\n\nNon-owner copy: pivotDevice = copy row l. Same argument within copy. Good.\n\nNow, is the copy's row l at iteration l exactly the owner's row k at iteration l? Induction: At block start, copy = owner's rows K after previous blocks. During each iteration, copy and owner both updated with identical formula and pivot row. Owner's pivot row for iteration l is its actual row l, which by induction equals copy row l. So yes. Good.\n\nNow, one more subtle issue: The owner's normal kernel updates rows K using `pivotDevice = d_dist row k`. But simultaneously (on the same stream sequentially) the kernel updates rows including future pivot rows l>current, which is fine; they will be used as pivot rows in later iterations. The copy update kernel also updates future pivot rows in the copy. Both consistent. Great.\n\nNow performance: Could we further reduce overhead by not launching the copy update kernel on the *owner* (done) and maybe on ranks? fine.\n\nOne inefficiency: non-owner ranks update their own rows kernel first, then copy kernel. The copy kernel is small and serialized after the big normal kernel, adding latency per iteration. Could we combine them into a single kernel launch that updates both d_dist local rows and d_pivotBlock rows? Since both use the same pivot row and are independent, a fused kernel could process both ranges; this would halve kernel launches and avoid serialization. For non-owner, total rows to update = localRows + bl. We can launch one kernel over `localRows + bl` rows, with a pointer mapping: first localRows rows from d_dist, next bl rows from d_pivotBlock. But the kernel needs two dist/path pointers and per-row selection. Since d_dist and d_pivotBlock are separate buffers, we can pass both and have threads decide based on i. However, vectorized kernel can branch per block row (i), which is uniform within a block's row. Implement a fused kernel:\n```\n__global__ void fwUpdateFusedVecKernel(unsigned* dist, unsigned* path, unsigned* blockDist,\n                                       const unsigned* pivotRow, int n, int rows, int blockRows, unsigned k)\n```\nwhere rows = localRows, blockRows = bl; total rows = rows + blockRows. Thread i in [0,total): if i<rows -> distRow=dist+i*n, pathRow=path+i*n; else -> distRow=blockDist+(i-rows)*n, pathRow=null. For block rows, no path. This fuses two kernels into one, reducing launch overhead and allowing better GPU utilization (more blocks). For owner, total rows=localRows only (blockRows=0).\nThis would be nice but adds another kernel variant (vec and scalar). Alternatively, for non-owner we could first launch copy update then normal; same count. The launch overhead is ~2us each, and per n iterations that's 2n vs n. For n=512, saves ~1ms; for n=4096, saves ~8ms. Worth maybe. But code complexity increases. Given time, maybe not necessary.\n\nAnother inefficiency: The owner's normal kernel does localRows = n/P rows per iteration, while non-owners do localRows + bl rows. Load imbalance: owner does less work. But the barrier at next block waits for slowest non-owner. For b small, bl << localRows, so imbalance ~b/localRows. For n=512, b=8, localRows=128 → 6%. Fine.\n\nNow, the reported performance for n=512 np=4 ~9-14ms, while np=1 2ms. The overhead is dominated by per-block barrier (64 blocks) and per-k kernel launches (512 normal + 512 copy = 1024 launches), plus H2D and GPU contention. Could reduce block count by increasing b to 32 (16 blocks) and accept more copy work; kernel launches unchanged; barrier reduced. For n=512, b=32: blocks per rank = 4, global 16; copy work per non-owner rank = 12 blocks * 32²*512 = 6.3M vs 1.57M with b=8. Extra 4.7M updates ~ maybe 0.1ms. Barrier saves 48 barriers * ~? maybe 5-10us = 0.25-0.5ms. H2D total same. Could help slightly. For n=512, b=64: blocks per rank=2, global 8; copy work 6 blocks*64²*512=12.6M extra; barrier saves more. Hmm. Hard to say. We can benchmark different b by patching quickly via env? We could add an environment variable to override blockSize for tuning, then remove. Or just compile variants in /tmp.
